# United Kingdom

"UK" redirects here. For other uses, see [UK (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_\(disambiguation\)) and [United Kingdom (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_\(disambiguation\)). Not to be confused with [England](9316.md) or [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain).

The **United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland**, commonly known as the **United Kingdom** (**UK**) or **Britain**,[^cite_note-29] is a country in [Northwestern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Europe), off the coast of [the continental mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_mainland). It comprises [England](9316.md), [Scotland](26994.md), [Wales](69894.md) and [Northern Ireland](21265.md),[^cite_note-30] with a population of over 69 million in 2024. The UK includes the island of [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain), the north-eastern part of the island of [Ireland](147575.md), and most of [the smaller islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_the_United_Kingdom) within the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles), covering 94,354 square miles (244,376 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-ONSArea-14] It shares [a land border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Ireland%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_border) with the [Republic of Ireland](14560.md) and is surrounded by the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md), the [North Sea](21179.md), the [English Channel](9230.md), the [Celtic Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Sea) and the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea), while maintaining sovereignty over the [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies) and the [British Overseas Territories](207114.md). The capital and largest city of England and the UK is [London](17867.md); [Edinburgh](9602.md), [Cardiff](5882.md) and [Belfast](5046.md) are the national capitals of Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland, respectively.&#x20;

The British Isles have been continuously inhabited since the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic). In AD 43 the [Roman conquest of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_conquest_of_Britain) began. The [Roman departure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_Roman_rule_in_Britain) between 383 and 410 was followed by [Anglo-Saxon settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_settlement_of_Britain) beginning around 450. In 1066 the [Normans conquered England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Conquest). Over the 17th century the role of the [British monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_monarchy) was reduced, particularly as a result of the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War). In 1707 the Kingdom of England and the [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) united under the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union) to create the [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain). The [Acts of Union 1800](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1800) incorporated the [Kingdom of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ireland) to create the [United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland) in 1801. Most of Ireland [seceded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Ireland) from the UK in 1922 as the [Irish Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Free_State), and the [Royal and Parliamentary Titles Act 1927](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_and_Parliamentary_Titles_Act_1927) created the present United Kingdom.&#x20;

The UK [became the first industrialised country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_industrialisation#Industrial_Revolution_in_Europe) and was the world's [foremost power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foremost_power) for the majority of the 19th and early 20th centuries, particularly during the *[Pax Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_Britannica)* between 1815 and 1914. The [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) was the leading [economic power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_power) for most of the 19th century, a position supported by [its agricultural prosperity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Agricultural_Revolution), its role as a dominant [trading nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_nation), a massive industrial capacity, [significant technological achievements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_British_innovations_and_discoveries_in_the_19th_century), and the rise of [19th-century London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th-century_London) as the world's principal financial centre. At its height in the 1920s, the empire encompassed around a quarter of the world's landmass and population, and was the [largest in history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires#Empires_at_their_greatest_extent). However, [its involvement in the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_the_First_World_War) and [in the Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_World_War_II) damaged [Britain's economic power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_British_Empire), and a global wave of [decolonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation) led to the independence of most British colonies.&#x20;

The UK is a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) and [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy)[^cite_note-32] with three distinct jurisdictions: [England and Wales](85441.md), [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_law), and [Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Northern_Ireland). Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland have their own governments and parliaments which control various [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom) matters. A [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with [an advanced economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_United_Kingdom), the UK ranks amongst the [largest economies by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and is one of the world's [largest exporters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports) and [importers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports). As a [nuclear state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_state) with one of the [highest defence budgets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditures), the UK maintains [one of the strongest militaries in Europe](31730.md). [British culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_Kingdom) is globally influential, notably in the [Anglosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglosphere) and [the Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations); [its soft power influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicisation) is observable in the legal and political systems of many [former colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_that_have_gained_independence_from_the_United_Kingdom), and in its exports [of language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_world), [literature](296550.md), [theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_United_Kingdom), [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_the_United_Kingdom), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_the_United_Kingdom), [media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_the_United_Kingdom), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_the_United_Kingdom). A [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power), the UK is part of [numerous international organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_the_United_Kingdom#International_organisations).&#x20;

## Etymology and terminology

See also: [Britain (place name)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britain_\(place_name\)) and [Terminology of the British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminology_of_the_British_Isles)

The [Acts of Union 1707](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707) declared that the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) and the [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) were "United into One Kingdom by the Name of Great Britain".[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-Acts_of_Union_1707-34] The term "United Kingdom" has occasionally been used for the former [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain), although its official name from 1707 to 1800 was simply "Great Britain".[^cite_note-35] The [Acts of Union 1800](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1800) formed the [United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland). Following the [partition of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Ireland) and the independence of the [Irish Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Free_State) in 1922, which left [Northern Ireland](21265.md) as the only part of the island of Ireland within the United Kingdom, the name was changed in 1927 to the "United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland".[^cite_note-36]&#x20;

Although the United Kingdom is a sovereign country, England, Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland are widely referred to as [countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-37] The UK Prime Minister's website has used the phrase "countries within a country" to describe it.[^cite_note-page823-38] Some statistical summaries, such as those for the twelve [NUTS 1 regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NUTS_of_the_United_Kingdom), refer to Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland as "regions".[^cite_note-39] Northern Ireland is also referred to as a "province".[^cite_note-40] With regard to Northern Ireland, the descriptive name used "can be controversial, with the choice often revealing one's political preferences".[^cite_note-41]&#x20;

The term "Great Britain" conventionally refers to the island of Great Britain, or politically to England, Scotland and Wales in combination.[^cite_note-42] It is sometimes used as a loose synonym for the United Kingdom as a whole.[^cite_note-43] The word *England* is occasionally used incorrectly to refer to the United Kingdom as a whole, a mistake principally made by people from outside the UK.[^cite_note-44]&#x20;

The term "Britain" is used as a synonym for [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain),[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-Britain-Col-46] but also sometimes for the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-Britain-Col-46] Usage is mixed: the [style guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Style_guide) of the [UK Government](25318118.md) prefers the term "UK" rather than "Britain" or "British" (except when referring to embassies[^cite_note-49]),[^cite_note-50] while other government documents acknowledge that both terms refer to the United Kingdom and that elsewhere "British government" is used at least as frequently as "United Kingdom government".[^cite_note-PCGN-uk-guide-51] The UK [Permanent Committee on Geographical Names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Committee_on_Geographical_Names_for_British_Official_Use) recognises "United Kingdom", "UK" and "U.K." as shortened and abbreviated geopolitical terms for the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland in its toponymic guidelines; it does not list "Britain" but notes that "it is only the one specific nominal term 'Great Britain' which invariably excludes Northern Ireland".[^cite_note-PCGN-uk-guide-51] The [BBC](19344654.md) historically preferred to use "Britain" as shorthand only for Great Britain, though the present style guide does not take a position except that "Great Britain" excludes Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-52]&#x20;

The adjective "British" is commonly used to refer to matters relating to the United Kingdom and is used in law to refer to United Kingdom citizenship and [nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_nationality_law).[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-56] People of the United Kingdom use several different terms to describe their national identity and may identify themselves as being [British](19097669.md), [English](18803164.md), [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people), [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_people), [Northern Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_of_Northern_Ireland) or [Irish](775859.md);[^cite_note-57] or as having a combination of different national identities.[^cite_note-58]&#x20;

## History

Main articles: [Formation of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_of_the_United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Northern_Ireland) and [History of the British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_British_Isles)

Further information: [History of the constitution of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_constitution_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [Timeline of British history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_British_history)

### Prior to the Treaty of Union

Main articles: [History of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_England), [History of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Wales), [History of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Scotland), and [History of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Ireland)

Further information: [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) and [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland)



[Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge) in [Wiltshire](51231.md) is a ring of stones, each about 4 m (13 ft) high, 2 m (7 ft) wide and 25 [tonnes](31185.md), erected 2400–2200 BC.

Settlement by [Cro-Magnons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cro-Magnon) of what is now the United Kingdom occurred in waves beginning by about 30,000 years ago.[^cite_note-59] The island has been continuously inhabited since the last retreat of ice around 11,500 years ago.[^cite_note-60] By the end of the [region's prehistoric period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Britain), the population is thought to have belonged largely to a culture termed [Insular Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Celts), comprising [Brittonic Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britons_\(historical\)) and [Gaelic Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaelic_Ireland).[^cite_note-61]&#x20;

The [Roman conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_conquest_of_Britain), beginning in AD 43, and the 400-year [rule of southern Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain), was followed by an invasion by [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) [Anglo-Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon) settlers, reducing the Brittonic area mainly [to what was to become Wales](69894.md#Post-Roman_era), [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall) and, until the latter stages of the Anglo-Saxon settlement, the [Hen Ogledd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hen_Ogledd) (northern England and parts of southern Scotland).[^cite_note-62] Most of the [region settled by the Anglo-Saxons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_England) became unified as the Kingdom of England in the 10th century.[^cite_note-63] [Gaelic speakers in north-west Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A1l_Riata) (with connections to the north-east of Ireland and traditionally supposed to have migrated from there in the 5th century)[^cite_note-64] united with the [Picts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picts) to create the [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) in the 9th century.[^cite_note-65]&#x20;

Photograph of the Baths showing a rectangular area of greenish water surrounded by yellow stone buildings with pillars. In the background is the tower of the abbey.

The [Roman Baths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Baths_\(Bath\)) in [Bath, Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath,_Somerset), are a well-preserved *[thermae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermae)* from [Roman Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain).

In 1066, the [Normans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normans) invaded England from northern France. After [conquering England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_conquest_of_England) they seized [large parts of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_invasion_of_Wales), [conquered much of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_invasion_of_Ireland) and were invited to settle in Scotland, bringing to each country [feudalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) on the Northern French model and [Norman-French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman-French) culture.[^cite_note-66] The [Anglo-Norman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Normans) [ruling class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruling_class) greatly influenced, but eventually assimilated with, the local cultures.[^cite_note-67] Subsequent [medieval English kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Plantagenet) completed the [conquest of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_Wales) and tried unsuccessfully [to annex Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_Scottish_Independence). Asserting its independence in the 1320 [Declaration of Arbroath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Arbroath), Scotland maintained its independence thereafter, albeit in [near-constant conflict with England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Scottish_Wars).[^cite_note-68]&#x20;

In 1215, [Magna Carta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Carta) was the first document to state that no government was above the law and that citizens have rights protecting them.[^cite_note-69]&#x20;

The English monarchs, through inheritance of [substantial territories in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angevin_Empire) and claims to the French crown, were also heavily involved in conflicts in France, most notably the [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years%27_War), while the [Kings of Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Scottish_monarchs) were in [an alliance with the French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Alliance) during this period.[^cite_note-70] [Early modern Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_Britain) saw religious conflict resulting from the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation) and the introduction of [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) state churches in each country.[^cite_note-71] The [English Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Reformation) ushered in political, constitutional, social and cultural change in the 16th century and [established](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) the [Church of England](5955.md). It defined a national identity for England and slowly, but profoundly, changed people's religious beliefs.[^cite_note-72] Wales was [fully incorporated into the Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_in_Wales_Acts_1535_and_1542),[^cite_note-73] and Ireland was constituted as a kingdom in personal union with the English crown.[^cite_note-74] During the [Plantation of Ulster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_of_Ulster) in the 17th century, the lands of the Catholic Gaelic nobility were confiscated by the Crown and granted to Protestant settlers from England and Scotland.[^cite_note-75]&#x20;



[James VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_VI_and_I), [King of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Scotland), succeeded to the English and Irish thrones in 1603.

The [Union of the Crowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns) occurred in 1603 when [James VI of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_VI_of_Scotland) inherited the thrones of England and Ireland, subsequently moving his court from Edinburgh to London. Although unified under a single monarch, the three realms remained entirely separate political entities, preserving their respective parliaments, judicial systems, and established churches.[^cite_note-76]&#x20;

In the mid-17th century, all three kingdoms [were involved in a series of connected wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_the_Three_Kingdoms) (including the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War)) which led to the temporary overthrow of the monarchy, with [the execution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Charles_I) of [King Charles I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_I_of_England), and the establishment of the short-lived [unitary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_republic) of the [Commonwealth of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_England), Scotland and Ireland.[^cite_note-77]&#x20;

Although the [monarchy was restored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_\(1660\)), the [Interregnum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interregnum_\(1649%E2%80%931660\)) along with the [Glorious Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glorious_Revolution) of 1688 and the subsequent [Bill of Rights 1689](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_of_Rights_1689) in England and [Claim of Right Act 1689](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claim_of_Right_Act_1689) in Scotland ensured that, unlike much of the rest of Europe, [royal absolutism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_absolutism) would not prevail, and a professed Catholic could never accede to the throne. The [British constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_constitution) would develop on the basis of [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) and the [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system).[^cite_note-78] With the founding of the [Royal Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society) in 1660, science was greatly encouraged. During this period, particularly in England, the development of [naval power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_navy) and an interest in [voyages of discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery) led to the acquisition and settlement of overseas colonies, particularly in North America and the Caribbean.[^cite_note-79]&#x20;

Though previous attempts at uniting the two kingdoms within Great Britain in 1606, 1667 and 1689 had proved unsuccessful, the attempt initiated in 1705 led to the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union) of 1706 being agreed and ratified by both parliaments.&#x20;

### Union of England and Scotland

Main articles: [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union) and [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain)



The [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union) which unified the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) and [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) on 1 May 1707

On 1 May 1707 the Kingdom of Great Britain was formed, the result of the [Acts of Union 1707](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707) between the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) and [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland).[^cite_note-80] In the 18th century cabinet government developed under [Robert Walpole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Walpole), who is considered the *[de facto](43594.md)* first [prime minister](24150.md) from 1721 to 1742. A series of [Jacobite uprisings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobite_uprisings) sought to remove the Protestant [House of Hanover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Hanover) from the throne and restore the Catholic [House of Stuart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Stuart). In 1746, the Jacobites were defeated at the [Battle of Culloden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Culloden), after which the [Scottish Highlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlanders) were forcibly assimilated into Scotland by revoking the feudal independence of [clan chiefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_clan_chief). The British colonies in North America that broke away in the [American War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) became the [United States](3434750.md). British imperial ambition turned towards Asia, particularly to [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj).[^cite_note-81]&#x20;

British merchants played a leading part in the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md), mainly between 1662 and 1807 when British or British-colonial [slave ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ships) transported nearly 3.3 million slaves from Africa.[^cite_note-82] The slaves were taken to [work on plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_economy), principally [in the Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_British_West_Indies) but also [in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America).[^cite_note-83] However, with pressure from the [abolitionist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_Kingdom), Parliament banned the trade in 1807, banned slavery in the British Empire in 1833, and Britain took a leading role in the movement to abolish slavery worldwide through the [blockade of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Africa) and pressing other nations to end their trade with a series of treaties.[^cite_note-84]&#x20;

### United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland

Main articles: [History of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_Kingdom), [Acts of Union 1800](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1800), and [United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland)



[Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria) reigned as [Queen of the United Kingdom](47063.md) and [Empress of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empress_of_India) during the 19th century.

In 1800, the parliaments of Great Britain and Ireland each passed an Act of Union, uniting the two kingdoms and creating the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland on 1 January 1801.[^cite_note-85]&#x20;

After the defeat of France at the end of the [French Revolutionary Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolutionary_Wars) and [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) (1792–1815), the United Kingdom emerged as the principal naval and imperial power (with London the [largest city in the world from about 1830](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cities_throughout_history)).[^cite_note-86] [Unchallenged at sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Royal_Navy_\(after_1707\)#Pax_Britannica,_1815%E2%80%931914), British dominance was later described as the *[Pax Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_Britannica)* ("British Peace"), a period of [relative peace amongst the great powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_relations_of_the_Great_Powers_\(1814%E2%80%931919\)) (1815–1914) during which the British Empire became the global [hegemon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegemon)[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] and adopted the role of [global policeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_policeman).[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] From 1853 to 1856, Britain took part in the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War), allied with the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) against [Tsarist Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire).[^cite_note-91] Following the [Indian Rebellion of 1857](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857) the British government led by [Lord Palmerston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_John_Temple,_3rd_Viscount_Palmerston) assumed [direct rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_rule) over [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj). Alongside the formal control it exerted over its own colonies, British dominance of much of world trade meant that it [effectively controlled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_empire) the economies of regions such as [East Asia](19605700.md) and [Latin America](18524.md).[^cite_note-92]&#x20;

Throughout the [Victorian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era) (1837–1901), political attitudes favoured [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) and *[laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire)* policies. Beginning with the [Great Reform Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Reform_Act) in 1832, Parliament gradually [widened the voting franchise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Acts), with the [1884 Reform Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1884) championed by [William Gladstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone) granting [suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffrage) to a majority of males for the first time. The British population increased at a dramatic rate, accompanied by rapid [urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation), causing significant social and economic stresses.[^cite_note-93] By the late 19th century, [Conservative governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) under [Benjamin Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli) and [Lord Salisbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury) accelerated [imperial expansion in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa), sustained a foreign policy of "[splendid isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Splendid_isolation)" from European alliances, and intensified strategic containment of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) in [Central Asia](6742.md), a competition known as the [Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Game).[^cite_note-94] During this time, [Canada](5042916.md), [Australia](4689264.md) and [New Zealand](4913064.md) were granted self-governing [dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion) status.[^cite_note-95] At the turn of the 20th century, Britain's industrial dominance became challenged by the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) and the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-96] The [Edwardian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwardian_era) (1901–1910) saw [social reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_welfare_reforms) and [home rule for Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule_movement) become important domestic issues, while the [Labour Party](19279158.md) emerged from an alliance of [trade unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_the_United_Kingdom) and small socialist groups in 1900, and [suffragettes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffragettes) campaigned for women's right to vote.[^cite_note-97]&#x20;

### World wars and partition of Ireland

Main articles: [History of the United Kingdom during the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_the_First_World_War), [Partition of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Ireland), [Interwar Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_Britain), [United Kingdom home front during the Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_home_front_during_the_Second_World_War), and [Military history of the United Kingdom during World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_World_War_II)



Wreaths being laid during the [Remembrance Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remembrance_Sunday) service at the [Cenotaph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cenotaph) in [Whitehall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitehall), London



A [Spitfire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermarine_Spitfire) and a [Hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_Hurricane) as flown in the [Battle of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Britain) during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War)

Britain was one of the principal [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) that defeated the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) in the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War) (1914–1918). Alongside their French, Russian and (after 1917) American counterparts,[^cite_note-98] British armed forces were engaged across much of the British Empire and in several regions of Europe, particularly on the [Western Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Front_\(World_War_I\)).[^cite_note-Westwell\&Cove-99] The high fatalities of [trench warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trench_warfare) caused the loss of much of a generation of men, with lasting social effects in the nation and a great disruption in the social order. Britain had suffered 2.5 million casualties and finished the war with a huge national debt.[^cite_note-Westwell\&Cove-99]&#x20;

The consequences of the war persuaded the government to expand the right to vote in national and local elections to all adult men and most adult women with the [Representation of the People Act 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1918).[^cite_note-Westwell\&Cove-99] After the war Britain became a permanent member of the Executive Council of the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) and received [a mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations_mandate) over a number of former German and [Ottoman](22278.md) colonies. Under the leadership of [David Lloyd George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lloyd_George), the British Empire reached its greatest extent, covering a fifth of the world's land surface and a quarter of its population.[^cite_note-100]&#x20;

By the mid-1920s, most of the British population could listen to [BBC](19344654.md) radio programmes.[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102] Experimental [television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_the_United_Kingdom) broadcasts [began in 1929](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_television#United_Kingdom) and the [first scheduled BBC Television Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_day_of_BBC_television) commenced in 1936.[^cite_note-103] The rise of [Irish nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_nationalism), and disputes within Ireland over the terms of [Irish Home Rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule), led eventually to the [partition of the island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Ireland) in 1921.[^cite_note-104] [A period of conflict in what is now Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Troubles_in_Ulster_\(1920%E2%80%931922\)) occurred from June 1920 until June 1922. The [Irish Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Free_State) became independent, initially with [Dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion) status in 1922, and [unambiguously independent in 1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931#Irish_Free_State). Northern Ireland remained part of the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-105] The [1928 Equal Franchise Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1928_Equal_Franchise_Act) gave women electoral equality with men in national elections. Strikes in the mid-1920s culminated in the [General Strike of 1926](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Strike_of_1926). Still suffering from the long-term economic effects of the First World War, Britain was severely impacted by the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression_in_the_United_Kingdom) (1929–1932), which intensified hardship in old industrial areas in traditional sectors, led to mass unemployment peaking near 3 million, and precipitated widespread political instability and social unrest. [A coalition government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Government_\(1931\)) was formed in 1931.[^cite_note-106]&#x20;

Nonetheless, Britain was described as "a very wealthy country, formidable in arms, ruthless in pursuit of its interests and sitting at the heart of a global production system."[^cite_note-Edgerton2012-107] After [Nazi Germany](21212.md)'s [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland) in 1939, Britain entered the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War). Despite the defeat of its European allies in the first year, Britain and its empire continued the war against Germany.[^cite_note-Edgerton2012-107]&#x20;

In 1940, the [Royal Air Force](25679.md) successfully defended British airspace by repelling the German *[Luftwaffe](17885.md)* in the [Battle of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Britain). Urban areas suffered heavy bombing during [the Blitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blitz). The [Grand Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Alliance_\(World_War_II\)) of Britain, the United States and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) formed in 1941, leading the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) against the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers). There were eventual hard-fought victories in the [Battle of the Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Atlantic), the [North Africa campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Africa_campaign) and the [Italian campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_campaign_\(World_War_II\)). British forces played important roles in the [Normandy landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings) of 1944 and the [liberation of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_of_Europe). The British Army led the [Burma campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burma_campaign) against Japan, and the [British Pacific Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Pacific_Fleet) fought Japan at sea. [British scientists contributed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_contribution_to_the_Manhattan_Project) to the American [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project), whose task was to build a [nuclear weapon](21785.md).[^cite_note-108]&#x20;

### Post-war 20th century

Main articles: [Post-war Britain (1945–1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-war_Britain_\(1945%E2%80%931979\)) and [Social history of post-war Britain (1945–1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_history_of_post-war_Britain_\(1945%E2%80%931979\))



The [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) at its territorial peak in 1921

The United Kingdom was one of the [Big Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Three_\(World_War_II\)) powers (with the United States and the Soviet Union) who met to plan the [post-war world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_II);[^cite_note-109] it drafted the [Declaration by United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_by_United_Nations) with the United States and became one of the five permanent members of the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md). It worked closely with the United States to establish the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), the [World Bank](36752.md) and [NATO](21133.md).[^cite_note-110] The war left the UK severely weakened and financially dependent on the American [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan),[^cite_note-111] but it was spared the total war that devastated [eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe).[^cite_note-112]&#x20;

In the immediate post-war years the [Labour government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Government_1945%E2%80%931951) under [Clement Attlee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_Attlee) initiated a radical programme of reforms, which significantly affected British society [in the following decades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-war_consensus).[^cite_note-113] Major industries and public utilities were [nationalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalised), a [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) was established, and a comprehensive, publicly funded healthcare system, the [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service), was created.[^cite_note-114] The rise of nationalism in the colonies coincided with Britain's much-diminished economic position after [its involvement in the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_the_First_World_War) and [in the Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_World_War_II), so that a policy of [decolonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation) was unavoidable.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117] Independence was granted to India and Pakistan in 1947.[^cite_note-118] Over the next three decades, most colonies of the British Empire gained their independence, and many became members of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations).[^cite_note-119]&#x20;

The UK was the third country to develop [a nuclear weapons arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_and_the_United_Kingdom), with its first atomic bomb test, [Operation Hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Hurricane), in 1952, although the post-war limitations of Britain's international role were illustrated by the [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis) of 1956. As a result of a shortage of workers in the 1950s, the government [encouraged immigration](1247786.md) from [Commonwealth countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_countries). In the following decades the UK became a more [multiracial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracialism) and [multicultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) society.[^cite_note-120] Despite rising living standards in the late 1950s and 1960s, the United Kingdom's economic performance was less successful than many of its main competitors such as France, [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) and Japan. In 1969, the United Kingdom was the first democratic nation to [lower its voting age to 18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1969).[^cite_note-121]&#x20;

In the decades-long process of [European integration](683704.md) the UK was a founding member of the [Western European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_European_Union), established with the [London and Paris Conferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_and_Paris_Conferences) in 1954. In 1960 the UK was one of the seven founding members of the [European Free Trade Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association) (EFTA), but in 1973 it left to join the [European Communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Communities) (EC). In a [1975 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_United_Kingdom_European_Communities_membership_referendum) 67 per cent voted to stay in it.[^cite_note-122] When the EC became the [European Union](9317.md) (EU) in 1993, the UK was one of the 12 founding member states.&#x20;

From the late 1960s, Northern Ireland experienced communal and [paramilitary](146730.md) violence, sometimes affecting other parts of the UK, known as [the Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Troubles). It is usually considered to have ended with the 1998 [Belfast "Good Friday" Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belfast_Agreement).[^cite_note-123] Following a period of widespread economic slowdown and industrial strife in the 1970s, the [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) government of the 1980s led by [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher) initiated a radical policy of [monetarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monetarism), [deregulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation), particularly of the financial sector (for example, the [Big Bang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang_\(financial_markets\)) in 1986) and labour markets, the sale of state-owned companies (privatisation), and the withdrawal of subsidies to others.[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

In 1982, [Argentina](18951905.md) invaded the British territories of [South Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Georgia_and_the_South_Sandwich_Islands) and the [Falkland Islands](7550182.md), leading to the 10-week [Falklands War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falklands_War) in which Argentine forces were defeated. The inhabitants of the islands strongly favour British sovereignty, expressed in a [2013 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Falkland_Islands_sovereignty_referendum). From 1984 the British economy was helped by the inflow of substantial [North Sea oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea_oil) revenues.[^cite_note-125] Another British Overseas Territory, [Gibraltar](7607314.md),[^cite_note-126] is [a key military base](36862736.md); a [referendum in 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Gibraltar_sovereignty_referendum) on shared sovereignty with Spain was rejected.&#x20;

By the late 20th century, major changes were made to the governance of the UK with the establishment of [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) administrations for Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-127] [The statutory incorporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Act_1998) followed acceptance of the [European Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights). The UK remained a [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_modern_great_powers#United_Kingdom) with global diplomatic and military influence and a leading role in the United Nations and NATO.[^cite_note-David_M._McCourt-128]&#x20;

### 21st century

Main articles: [Political history of the United Kingdom (1979–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_history_of_the_United_Kingdom_\(1979%E2%80%93present\)) and [Social history of the United Kingdom (1979–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_history_of_the_United_Kingdom_\(1979%E2%80%93present\))



Prime Minister [Boris Johnson](19065069.md) signs the [Brexit withdrawal agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement) in 2020, formally [withdrawing the UK](41688778.md) from the [European Union](9317.md) (EU).

The United Kingdom broadly supported the United States' approach to the "[war on terror](13425800.md)" in the early 21st century.[^cite_note-129] British troops fought in the [war in Afghanistan](19666611.md), but controversy surrounded Britain's [military deployment in Iraq](5043324.md), which saw the [largest protest in British history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15_February_2003_anti-war_protests) in opposition to the government led by [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair).[^cite_note-130]&#x20;

The [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) (2007–2010) severely affected the British economy,[^cite_note-Latest_UK_GDP_data-131] and was followed by a period of weak growth and stagnation.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] The [Cameron–Clegg coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron%E2%80%93Clegg_coalition) government of 2010 introduced [austerity measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_government_austerity_programme) intended to tackle the substantial public deficits.[^cite_note-134] [A referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Scottish_independence_referendum) on [Scottish independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_independence) in 2014 resulted in the Scottish electorate voting by 55.3 to 44.7 per cent to remain part of the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-135]&#x20;

In 2016, 51.9 per cent of voters in the UK [voted to leave the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum) (EU).[^cite_note-136] The [UK left the EU](41688778.md) in 2020.[^cite_note-137] On 1 May 2021 the [EU–UK Trade and Cooperation Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU%E2%80%93UK_Trade_and_Cooperation_Agreement), a free trade agreement between the UK and the EU, came into force.[^cite_note-commission.europa.eu-138][^cite_note-139]&#x20;

The [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom) had a severe [impact on the British economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom), caused major [disruptions to education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_education_in_the_United_Kingdom) and had [far-reaching impacts on society and politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom) in 2020 and 2021.[^cite_note-GOVUK-140][^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] The United Kingdom was the first country in the world to use an approved [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md). Developing a [vaccine in the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford%E2%80%93AstraZeneca_COVID-19_vaccine) allowed the rollout to be amongst the fastest in the world.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144]&#x20;

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_the_United_Kingdom), [Fauna of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Great_Britain), and [Flora of Great Britain and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland)



A satellite image of the United Kingdom excluding [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland)

The total area of the United Kingdom is approximately 94,354 square miles (244,376 km<sup>2</sup>),[^cite_note-ONSArea-14][^cite_note-ONS_Standard_Area_Measurement-15] with a land area of 93,723 square miles (242,741 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-ONS_Standard_Area_Measurement-15] It occupies the major part of the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles)[^cite_note-145] and includes the island of Great Britain, the north-eastern one-sixth of the island of Ireland and [some smaller surrounding islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_the_United_Kingdom), meaning it comprises [England](9316.md), [Scotland](26994.md), [Wales](69894.md) and [Northern Ireland](21265.md).[^cite_note-ONS_Geography_Guide-146]&#x20;

Geographically, the United Kingdom lies between the [North Atlantic Ocean](698.md) and the [North Sea](21179.md) with the southeast coast coming within 22 miles (35 km) of the coast of northern France, from which it is separated by the [English Channel](9230.md).[^cite_note-factbook-147]&#x20;

The nearby island polities of the [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man), [Jersey](15693.md) and [Guernsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guernsey) are [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies), in union with the [British monarch](47063.md), but do not strictly form part of the United Kingdom or any of its three jurisdictions or four countries, although the British government retains responsibility for their external affairs. The Isle of Man lies roughly midway between Great Britain and the [island of Ireland](147575.md) in the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea), while the Channel Islands lie just off the northern French coast.&#x20;

The [Royal Greenwich Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Observatory,_Greenwich) in London was chosen as the defining point of the [Prime Meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Meridian)[^cite_note-148] at the [International Meridian Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Meridian_Conference) in 1884.[^cite_note-149]&#x20;

The UK lies between latitudes [49°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north) and [61° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/61st_parallel_north), and longitudes [9° W](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_meridian_west) and [2° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_meridian_east). Northern Ireland shares a 310-mile (499 km) land boundary with the Republic of Ireland[^cite_note-factbook-147] and has a 404-mile (650 km) coastline.[^cite_note-NI_coastline-150] The length of coastline of Great Britain plus its principal islands is about 19,491 miles (31,368 km) long, with the coastline of the main island Great Britain being 11,073 miles (17,820 km) of that,[^cite_note-UK_coastline-151] though measurements can vary greatly due to the [coastline paradox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastline_paradox).[^cite_note-152] It is connected to continental Europe by the [Channel Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Tunnel), which at 31 miles (50 km) (24 miles (38 km) underwater) is the longest underwater tunnel in the world.[^cite_note-153]&#x20;

The UK contains four terrestrial [ecoregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecoregion): [Celtic broadleaf forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_broadleaf_forests), [English Lowlands beech forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Lowlands_beech_forests), [North Atlantic moist mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_moist_mixed_forests), and [Caledonian conifer forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caledonian_Forest).[^cite_note-154] The area of woodland in the UK was estimated to be 3.25 million hectares in 2023, which represents 13 per cent of its land area.[^cite_note-155]&#x20;

### Climate

Main articles: [Climate of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [Climate change in the United Kingdom](25369297.md)

Further information: [Climate of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_England), [Climate of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Scotland), [Climate of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Wales), and [Climate of Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Northern_Ireland)

Most of the United Kingdom has a temperate climate, with generally cool temperatures and plentiful rainfall all year round.[^cite_note-factbook-147] The temperature varies with the seasons rarely dropping below 0 [°C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celsius) (32 [°F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahrenheit)) or rising above 30 °C (86 °F).[^cite_note-156] Some parts, away from the coast, of upland England, Wales, Northern Ireland and most of Scotland, experience a [subpolar oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subpolar_oceanic_climate). Higher elevations in Scotland experience a [continental subarctic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic_climate) and the mountains experience a [tundra climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra_climate).[^cite_note-157]&#x20;

The prevailing wind is from the southwest and bears frequent spells of mild and wet weather from the Atlantic Ocean,[^cite_note-factbook-147] although the eastern parts are mostly sheltered from this wind. Since the majority of the rain falls over the western regions, the eastern parts are the driest. Atlantic currents, warmed by the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream), bring mild winters, especially in the west where winters are wet and even more so over high ground. Summers are warmest in the southeast of England and coolest in the north. Heavy snowfall can occur in winter and early spring on high ground, and occasionally settles to great depth away from the hills.[^cite_note-158]&#x20;

The average total annual sunshine in the United Kingdom was 1,339.7 hours between 1971 and 2000.[^cite_note-ukaverages-159] The hours of sunshine vary from 1,200 to about 1,580 hours per year.[^cite_note-met_off_climate_series-160]&#x20;

Climate change has serious impacts on the country. A third of food price rise in 2023 was attributed to it.[^cite_note-161] In 2024 the United Kingdom ranked 5th out of 180 countries in the [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index).[^cite_note-162] A law has been passed that [UK greenhouse gas emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_by_the_United_Kingdom) will be [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_zero) by 2050.[^cite_note-163]&#x20;

### Topography



The United Kingdom's topography

[England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_England) accounts for 53 per cent of the UK, covering 50,350 square miles (130,395 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-164] Most of the country consists of lowland terrain,[^cite_note-Atlapedia-165] with upland and mountainous terrain northwest of the [Tees–Exe line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tees%E2%80%93Exe_line) which roughly divides the UK into [lowland and upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowland_and_upland) areas. Lowland areas include [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall), the [New Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Forest), the [South Downs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Downs) and the [Norfolk Broads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Broads). Upland areas include the [Lake District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_District), the [Pennines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennines), the [Yorkshire Dales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_Dales), [Exmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exmoor) and [Dartmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dartmoor). The main rivers and estuaries are the [Thames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Thames), [Severn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Severn), and the [Humber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humber). England's highest mountain is [Scafell Pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scafell_Pike), at 978 metres (3,209 ft) in the Lake District; its largest island is the [Isle of Wight](15102.md).&#x20;

[Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Scotland) accounts for 32 per cent of the UK, covering 30,410 square miles (78,772 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-166] This includes [nearly 800 islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Scotland),[^cite_note-167] notably the [Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrides), [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney) Islands and [Shetland Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland_Islands). Scotland is the most mountainous constituent country of the UK. The [Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands) to the north and west are the more rugged region containing the majority of Scotland's mountainous land, including the [Cairngorms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairngorms), [Loch Lomond and The Trossachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Lomond_and_The_Trossachs) and [Ben Nevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Nevis) which at 1,345 metres (4,413 ft)[^cite_note-168] is the highest point in the British Isles.[^cite_note-169]&#x20;

[Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Wales) accounts for less than 9 per cent of the UK, covering 8,020 square miles (20,779 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-170] It is mostly mountainous, though [South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Wales) is less mountainous than [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Wales) and [Mid Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid_Wales). The highest mountains in Wales are in [Snowdonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowdonia) and include [Snowdon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowdon) ([Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language): *Yr Wyddfa*) which, at 1,085 metres (3,560 ft), is the highest peak in Wales.[^cite_note-Atlapedia-165] Wales has over 1,680 miles (2,704 kilometres) of coastline including the [Pembrokeshire Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pembrokeshire_Coast).[^cite_note-UK_coastline-151] Several islands lie off the Welsh mainland, the largest of which is [Anglesey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglesey) (*Ynys Môn*).&#x20;

[Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Ireland), separated from Great Britain by the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea) and [North Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Channel_\(Great_Britain_and_Ireland\)), has an area of 5,470 square miles (14,160 km<sup>2</sup>) and is mostly hilly. It includes [Lough Neagh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lough_Neagh) which, at 150 square miles (388 km<sup>2</sup>), is the largest lake in the British Isles by area,[^cite_note-171] [Lough Erne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lough_Erne), which has over 150 islands, and the [Giant's Causeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant%27s_Causeway), which is listed by [UNESCO](21786641.md) as a [World Heritage Site](44940.md). The highest peak in Northern Ireland is [Slieve Donard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slieve_Donard) in the [Mourne Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mourne_Mountains) at 852 metres (2,795 ft).[^cite_note-Atlapedia-165]&#x20;

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Politics of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_the_United_Kingdom), [Government of the United Kingdom](25318118.md), and [Monarchy of the United Kingdom](47063.md)

Further information: [Constitution of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_Kingdom)



[Charles III](125248.md)\
[King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarch_of_the_United_Kingdom)



[Andy Burnham](414818.md)\
[Prime Minister](24150.md)

The United Kingdom is a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) and a [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy)[^cite_note-172] operating under the [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system), otherwise known as a "democratic parliamentary monarchy".[^cite_note-173] It is a [centralised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centralized_government), [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state)[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175] wherein the [Parliament of the United Kingdom](13964.md) is [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_sovereignty_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-176] Parliament is made up of the elected [House of Commons](13828.md), the appointed [House of Lords](13658.md) and [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown) (as personified by [the monarch](47063.md)).[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-parliament.uk-180] The main business of Parliament takes place in the two houses,[^cite_note-parliament.uk-180] but [royal assent](153067.md) is required for a bill to become an [act of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Parliament_\(UK\)) (that is, [statute law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_law)).[^cite_note-181] As a result of parliamentary sovereignty, the [British constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_Kingdom) is [uncodified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncodified_constitution), consisting mostly of disparate written sources, including parliamentary [statutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute), judge-made [case law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_law) and international treaties, together with [constitutional conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Constitution#Conventions).[^cite_note-182] Nevertheless, the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_Kingdom) recognises a number of principles underlying the British constitution, such as parliamentary sovereignty, the [rule of law](25166191.md), [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) and upholding [international law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-183]&#x20;

[King Charles III](125248.md) is the [monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarch) and [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) of the United Kingdom and 14 other independent [sovereign states](23604120.md), referred to as "[Commonwealth realms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_realm)". The monarch is formally vested with all executive authority as the personal embodiment of [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown) and is "fundamental to the law and working of government in the UK".[^cite_note-184] The disposition of such powers however, including those belonging to the [royal prerogative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative_in_the_United_Kingdom), is generally exercised only on the [advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_\(constitutional_law\)) of [ministers of the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministers_of_the_Crown) responsible to Parliament and thence to the electorate. Nevertheless, in the performance of official duties the monarch has "the right to be consulted, the right to encourage, and the right to warn".[^cite_note-185] In addition the monarch has a number of [reserve powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_power#United_Kingdom) at his disposal to uphold [responsible government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsible_government) and prevent [constitutional crises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_crisis).[^cite_note-187]&#x20;

The prime minister is the [head of government](37570.md) in the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-188] Acting under the direction and supervision of a [Cabinet](162790.md) of senior ministers selected and led by the prime minister, [His Majesty's Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Majesty%27s_Government) serves as the principal instrument for public policymaking, administers public services and, through the [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom), promulgates [statutory instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutory_instrument_\(UK\)) and tenders advice to the monarch.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] Nearly all prime ministers have served concurrently as [First Lord of the Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Treasury)[^cite_note-192] and all prime ministers have continuously served as First Lord of the Treasury since 1905,[^cite_note-193] [Minister for the Civil Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_the_Civil_Service) since 1968,[^cite_note-194] and [Minister for the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_the_Union) since 2019.[^cite_note-195] While appointed by the monarch, in modern times the prime minister is, by [convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_conventions_of_the_United_Kingdom), an MP, the leader of the political party with the most seats in the House of Commons, and holds office by virtue of their ability to [command the confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_motions_in_the_United_Kingdom) of the House of Commons.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] The prime minister as of 20 July 2026 is [Andy Burnham](414818.md), the [leader of the Labour Party](19043368.md).&#x20;

Although not part of the United Kingdom, the three [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies) of [Jersey](15693.md), [Guernsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guernsey) and the [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man), as well as the 14 [British Overseas Territories](207114.md), are subject to the sovereignty of the British Crown.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

[Democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) was found by the 2026 [V-Dem Democracy Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute#Democracy_Report) for the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-j496-199]&#x20;

### Elections

Main article: [Elections in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Large sand-coloured building of Gothic design beside brown river. The building has several large towers, including large clock tower.

The [Palace of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Westminster) in London is the seat of both houses of the Parliament of the United Kingdom.

For [general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_elections_in_the_United_Kingdom) (elections to the House of Commons), the United Kingdom is divided into 650 [constituencies](1784582.md), each of which is represented by one [member of Parliament](3776360.md) (MP) elected by the [first-past-the-post](261709.md) system.[^cite_note-parliament.uk-2-200] MPs hold office for up to five years and must then stand for re-election if they wish to continue to be an MP.[^cite_note-parliament.uk-2-200] The [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), colloquially known as the Tory Party or the Tories, and the [Labour Party](19279158.md) have been the dominant political parties in the country since the 1920s, leading to the UK being described as a [two-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system). However, since the 1920s other [political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_the_United_Kingdom) have won seats in the House of Commons, although never more than the Conservatives or Labour.[^cite_note-201] The 2020s marked the erosion of the two-party system and the growth of a multi-party system.[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Countries of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom), [Administrative geography of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_geography_of_the_United_Kingdom), and [List of counties in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_the_United_Kingdom)



The four countries of the United Kingdom

The geographical division of the United Kingdom into counties or [shires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire) began in England and Scotland in the [early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages), and was completed throughout Great Britain and Ireland by the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period).[^cite_note-203] Modern [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government) by elected councils, partly based on the ancient counties, was established by separate Acts of Parliament: in England and Wales [in 1888](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_Act_1888), Scotland [in 1889](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_\(Scotland\)_Act_1889) and Ireland [in 1898](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_\(Ireland\)_Act_1898), meaning there is no consistent system of administrative or geographic demarcation across the UK,[^cite_note-204] and [England and Wales](85441.md), [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_law) and [Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Northern_Ireland) each have their own distinct jurisdictions.[^cite_note-205] Until the 19th century there was little change to those arrangements, but there has since been a constant evolution of role and function.[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

[Local government in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England) is complex, with the distribution of functions varying according to local arrangements. The upper-tier [subdivisions of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_England) are the nine [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_England), used primarily for statistical purposes.[^cite_note-207] One of the regions, [Greater London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_London_Authority), has had a directly elected assembly and mayor since 2000 following popular support for the proposal in a [1998 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Greater_London_Authority_referendum).[^cite_note-208]&#x20;

[Local government in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Scotland) is divided into [32 council areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Scotland) with a wide variation in size and population. The cities of [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow), Edinburgh, [Aberdeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberdeen) and [Dundee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dundee) are separate council areas, as is the [Highland Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_the_Highland_council_area), which includes a third of Scotland's area but only just over 200,000 people. Local councils are made up of elected councillors, of whom there are 1,223.[^cite_note-political-studies-association-209]&#x20;

[Local government in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Wales) consists of 22 [unitary authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authority), each led by a leader and cabinet elected by the council itself. These include the cities of Cardiff, [Swansea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swansea) and [Newport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newport,_Wales), which are unitary authorities in their own right.[^cite_note-unitary-210] Elections are held every four years under the first-past-the-post system.[^cite_note-unitary-210]&#x20;

[Local government in Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Northern_Ireland) since 2015 has been organised into 11 district councils, each elected by single transferable vote. Their powers are limited to services such as waste collection, dog control, and maintaining parks and cemeteries.[^cite_note-Devenport-211] This system was introduced after a 2008 decision by the executive to replace the previous structure of 26 districts which had been in place since 1973.[^cite_note-212]&#x20;

### Devolution

Main article: [Devolution in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Devolved, reserved and excepted matters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolved,_reserved_and_excepted_matters)



Prime Minister Keir Starmer meets with the first ministers of Scotland, Northern Ireland and Wales during the [Council of Nations and Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Nations_and_Regions) summit.

In the United Kingdom a process of devolution has transferred various powers from the UK Government to three of the four UK countries—Scotland, Northern Ireland and Wales, which since 1999, have their own governments and parliaments that control various devolved matters.[^cite_note-213] These powers vary and have been moved to the [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government), the [Welsh Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Government), the [Northern Ireland Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Executive) and (in England) to [strategic authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_authorities): the [Greater London Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_London_Authority), [combined authorities, and combined county authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authorities_and_combined_county_authorities).[^cite_note-214] Amongst the devolved parliaments across the United Kingdom, the [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) has the most extensive responsibilities for [devolved powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolved,_reserved_and_excepted_matters), and has been described as "one of the most powerful devolved parliaments in the world".[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]&#x20;

The United Kingdom has an [uncodified constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncodified_constitution) and constitutional matters are not amongst the powers that have been devolved. Under the doctrine of [parliamentary sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_sovereignty_in_the_United_Kingdom), the UK Parliament could, in theory, therefore, abolish the Scottish Parliament, Senedd or Northern Ireland Assembly.[^cite_note-217] Though in the [Scotland Act 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Act_2016) and the [Wales Act 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wales_Act_2017) it states that the Scottish Government and the Welsh Government "are a permanent part of the United Kingdom's constitutional arrangements".[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219]&#x20;

In practice it would be politically difficult for the UK Parliament to abolish devolution to the Scottish Parliament and the Senedd because these institutions were created by referendums.[^cite_note-220] The political constraints placed upon the UK Parliament's power to interfere with devolution in Northern Ireland are greater still, because devolution in Northern Ireland rests upon an international agreement with the [Government of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Ireland).[^cite_note-221] The UK Parliament restricts the three devolved parliaments' legislative powers in economic policy matters through [an act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_Internal_Market_Act_2020) passed in 2020.[^cite_note-UKIM-222]&#x20;

#### England

Unlike Scotland, Northern Ireland and Wales, England does not have a separate devolved government or national parliament,[^cite_note-223] rather a process of devolution of powers from the central government to local authorities has taken place, first in 1998.[^cite_note-224] The [Greater London Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_London_Authority) was set up following a [referendum in 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Greater_London_Authority_referendum). Colloquially known as City Hall, it is the devolved regional government body for Greater London. It consists of two political branches: a directly elected [Mayor of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_London) and the [London Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Assembly), which serves as a check and balance on the Mayor.&#x20;

A [combined authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authorities_and_combined_county_authorities) is a type of local government institution introduced in England outside Greater London by the [Local Democracy, Economic Development and Construction Act 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Democracy,_Economic_Development_and_Construction_Act_2009). CAs allow a group of local authorities to pool appropriate responsibility and receive certain devolved functions from central government to deliver transport and economic policy more effectively over a wider area.[^cite_note-225] A combined county authority is a similar type of local-government institution introduced in England outside Greater London by the [Levelling-up and Regeneration Act 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levelling-up_and_Regeneration_Act_2023), but may only be formed by upper-tier authorities: [county councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_council) and [unitary authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authority).[^cite_note-226]&#x20;

#### Scotland

Main article: [Responsibilities of the Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsibilities_of_the_Scottish_Government)



Prime Minister Keir Starmer meets with [First Minister of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Minister_of_Scotland) [John Swinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Swinney), the head of the [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government), at [Bute House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bute_House), Edinburgh.
