### Ethnicity

Main article: [Ethnic groups in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Historically, indigenous British people were thought to be [descended from ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_the_British_Isles) that settled there before the 12th century: the [Celts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts), Romans, Anglo-Saxons, Norse and the [Normans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normans). [Welsh people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_people) could be the oldest ethnic group in the UK.[^cite_note-379] The UK has a history of non-white immigration with [Liverpool](18081.md) having the oldest black population in the country dating from at least the 1730s,[^cite_note-380] in addition to having the oldest [Chinese](1255834.md) community in Europe dating from the 19th century.[^cite_note-381]&#x20;

In 2021, 83.1 per cent of the UK population was classified as white, 8.6 per cent as Asian or Asian British, 3.7 per cent as Black or Black British, 2.7 per cent as mixed ethnicity, and 2.0 per cent other.[^cite_note-Ethnicity2021-7] Ethnic diversity varies significantly across the UK. In 2021, 46.2 per cent of London's population was from a non-white ethnic minority,[^cite_note-Regionaldiversity-382] compared less than 10 per cent in the South West and North East of England, Wales,[^cite_note-Regionaldiversity-382] Scotland[^cite_note-383] and Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-384]&#x20;

### Language

Main article: [Languages of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_United_Kingdom)

The [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) is the *[de facto](43594.md)* official and most widely spoken language in the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386] The UK promotes the language globally to build connections, understanding and trust between people in the UK and countries worldwide.[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388]&#x20;

In the UK, the English language is spoken with distinctive characteristics that collectively form what is known as [British English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English). The variety of dialects and accents is often noted, with nearby regions frequently having highly distinct variations. [Received Pronunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Received_Pronunciation) is traditionally associated with educated speakers in southern England.[^cite_note-389] The main national dialects are [Scottish English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_English), [Welsh English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_English) and [Northern Irish English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Irish_English). Distinctive regional varieties include [Brummie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brummie_dialect), [Cockney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cockney), [Geordie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geordie), [Mancunian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_dialect), [Scouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scouse), [West Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Country_English), [Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_dialect) and [MLE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multicultural_London_English) (Multicultural London English).[^cite_note-390]&#x20;



[Bilingual sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilingual_sign) ([Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_language) and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language)) in [Newry](352560.md), Northern Ireland

Three indigenous [Celtic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_languages) are spoken in the UK: [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language), [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_language) and [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic). [Cornish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_language), which became extinct as a first language in the late-18th century, is being revived and has a small group of second-language speakers.[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-reglang-3] In the [2021 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census) the [Welsh-speaking population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh-speaking_population) of Wales aged three or older was 538,300 (17.8 per cent).[^cite_note-392] In addition it is estimated that about 200,000 Welsh-speakers live in England.[^cite_note-393] In the 2021 census in Northern Ireland 12.4 per cent of people had some [Irish language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_language_in_Northern_Ireland) ability and 10.4 per cent had some facility in the [Ulster-Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster_Scots_dialect) language.[^cite_note-394]&#x20;

In 2001, over 92,000 people in Scotland, just under 2 per cent of the population, had some Gaelic language ability, including 72 per cent of those living in the [Outer Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Hebrides).[^cite_note-395] The number of children being taught either Welsh or Scottish Gaelic is increasing.[^cite_note-396] [Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_language), a language descended from early northern [Middle English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_English), has limited [recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Charter_for_Regional_or_Minority_Languages) alongside its regional variant, [Ulster Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster_Scots_dialects) in Northern Ireland, without specific commitments to protection and promotion.[^cite_note-reglang-3][^cite_note-397] As of April 2020 there are around 151,000 users of [British Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Sign_Language) (BSL), a [sign language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sign_language) used by deaf people, in the UK.[^cite_note-398]&#x20;

In 2013, it was estimated that 95 per cent of the UK's population were [monolingual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monolingual) English speakers.[^cite_note-BBC_languages-399] In 2013, over 5 per cent of the population were estimated to speak languages brought to the UK as a result of immigration.[^cite_note-BBC_languages-399] [South Asian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_languages) are the largest group, which includes [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language), [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu), [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language), [Sylheti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylheti_language), [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi), [Pahari-Pothwari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahari-Pothwari), [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language) and [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language).[^cite_note-400] In the 2011 census [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) was the second-largest language spoken in England, with 546,000 speakers.[^cite_note-401] In 2019 some three-quarters of a million people spoke little or no English.[^cite_note-402]&#x20;

### Religion

Main articles: [Religion in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [Freedom of religion in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_the_United_Kingdom)



The [Westminster Abbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_Abbey) in [London](17867.md). This was the [coronation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_British_monarch) site of 40 monarchs, 18 royal burials, and at least 16 royal weddings since 1100.

[Christianity](5211.md) has dominated religious life in the United Kingdom for [more than 1,400 years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_Britain).[^cite_note-403] Although a majority of citizens still identify with Christianity in surveys, regular church attendance has fallen dramatically since the middle of the 20th century,[^cite_note-404] while immigration and demographic change have contributed to the growth of Islam and other faiths.[^cite_note-405] This has led some commentators to describe the UK as a multi-faith,[^cite_note-406] [secularised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularised),[^cite_note-407] or [post-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Christian) society.[^cite_note-408]&#x20;

In the 2021 census of England and Wales, 46.2 per cent of respondents reported that they were Christians, with the next largest faiths being [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_Kingdom) (6.5 per cent), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_the_United_Kingdom) (1.7 per cent), [Sikhism](7940789.md) (0.9 per cent), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_United_Kingdom) (0.5 per cent), [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_in_the_United_Kingdom) (0.5 per cent), and all other religions (0.6 per cent). Of the respondents, 37.2 per cent stated that they had [no religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_the_United_Kingdom) and a further 6.0 per cent did not answer the optional question.[^cite_note-409] In the census of Northern Ireland, 42.3 per cent of the population was recorded as Catholic, 37.7% as Protestant or other Christian, 1.3 per cent as belonging to other religions, 17.4 per cent as having no religion and 1.6 per cent did not state a religion.[^cite_note-410] In Scotland's 2022 census, 51.1 per cent of the population stated that they had no religion, 20.4 per cent were recorded as Church of Scotland, 13.3 per cent as Catholic, 5.1 per cent as other Christian, 2.2 per cent as Muslim and 0.6 per cent as Hindu, with 1.2 per cent belonging to other religions and 6.2 per cent not stating a religion.[^cite_note-411]&#x20;

A [Tearfund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tearfund) survey in 2007 showed that one Briton in ten attends church weekly.[^cite_note-412] Between the 2001 and 2011 censuses there was a 12 per cent decrease in those who identified as Christian, while the percentage reporting no religious affiliation doubled. This contrasted with growth in the other main religious group categories, with the number of [Muslims](19541.md) increasing the most to about 5 per cent.[^cite_note-413]&#x20;

The [Church of England](5955.md) is the [established church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion) in England.[^cite_note-414] It retains [representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lords_Spiritual) in the UK Parliament, and the British monarch is its [Supreme Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Governor_of_the_Church_of_England).[^cite_note-415] In [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Scotland) the [Church of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Scotland) is the [national church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_church). It is not subject to state control, and the British monarch is an ordinary member, required to swear an oath to "maintain and preserve the Protestant Religion and [Presbyterian Church Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_polity)" upon his or her accession.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-reglang-3][^cite_note-417] The [Church in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_in_Wales) was disestablished in 1920 and, because the [Church of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Ireland) was disestablished in 1870 before the [partition of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_Ireland), there is no established church in Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-418] Although there are no UK-wide data in the 2001 census on adherence to individual Christian denominations, it has been estimated that 62 per cent of Christians are Anglican, 13.5 per cent Catholic, 6 per cent Presbyterian, and 3.4 per cent Methodist, with smaller numbers of other denominations.[^cite_note-419]&#x20;

### Migration

Main articles: [Historical immigration to Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_immigration_to_Great_Britain) and [Modern immigration to the United Kingdom](1247786.md)

See also: [Foreign-born population of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-born_population_of_the_United_Kingdom)



Estimated foreign-born population by country of birth from April 2007 to March 2008



Estimated number of British citizens living overseas by country in 2006:&#x20;

&#x20; Greater than or equal to 1,000,000

&#x20; 500,000 - 999,999

&#x20; 100,000 - 499,999

&#x20; 50,000 - 99,999

&#x20; 10,000 - 49,999

&#x20; 5,000 - 9,999

&#x20; 1,000 - 4,999

&#x20; 100 - 999

&#x20; Less than 100 or no data

&#x20; UK

Immigration is contributing to a rising population in the United Kingdom,[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-bbc-23-05-2024-421] with arrivals and UK-born children of migrants accounting for about half of the population increase between 1991 and 2001. According to statistics released in 2015, 27 per cent of UK live births in 2014 were to mothers born outside the UK.[^cite_note-422]&#x20;

In 2013 approximately 208,000 foreign nationals were naturalised as British citizens, the highest number since 1962. This figure fell to around 125,800 in 2014. Between 2009 and 2013 the average number of British citizenships granted annually was 195,800. The most common origins of those naturalised in 2024 were Pakistani, Indian, [Nigerian](21383.md), [Filipino](23440.md), [Bangladeshi](3454.md), Italian, Turkish, Romanian and Iranian. The number of grants of settlement, which confer [permanent residence](1284324.md) in the UK but not [citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_citizenship),[^cite_note-423] was approximately 154,700 in 2013, higher than the previous two years.[^cite_note-Blinder2015-424] Long-term net migration (the number of people immigrating minus the number emigrating) reached a record of 860,000 in 2023, with immigration at 1.326 million and emigration at 466,000. In comparison, in 2024 net migration was estimated to be 431,000 with immigration at 948,000 and emigration at 517,000.[^cite_note-425]&#x20;

[Emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_diaspora) was an important feature of British society in the 19th century. Between 1815 and 1930, around 11.4 million people emigrated from Britain and 7.3 million from Ireland.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-richards-2018-427] Estimates show that by the end of the 20th century, some 300 million people of British and Irish descent were permanently settled around the globe.[^cite_note-richards-2018-427] In 2006 at least 5.5 million UK-born people lived abroad,[^cite_note-BritsAbroad-428][^cite_note-429] mainly in Australia, Spain, the United States and Canada.[^cite_note-BritsAbroad-428][^cite_note-430]&#x20;

### Education

Main articles: [Education in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [Universities in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universities_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Education in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_England), [Education in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Scotland), [Education in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Wales), and [Education in Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Northern_Ireland)

Education in the United Kingdom is a [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) matter, with each country having a separate education system. About 38 per cent of the United Kingdom population has a university or [college degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_degree), the highest percentage in Europe and one of the highest in the world.[^cite_note-431] The UK is home to [many universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_the_United_Kingdom), including the [University of Oxford](31797.md) and [University of Cambridge](25978572.md), which often achieve first place on global rankings.[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433]&#x20;

University education has varied [tuition fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loans_and_grants_in_the_United_Kingdom) in different regions of the UK. England and Wales have a fixed maximum annual fee for all UK citizens, contingent on attaining a certain level of income. Only those who reach a certain salary threshold pay this fee through [general taxation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-434] Northern Ireland and Scotland have a reduced maximum fee or no fee for citizens where it is their home region. Some NHS courses have bursaries which pay the fee and in 2017 it was stated that each doctor gets subsidised by £230,000 during their training.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436]&#x20;

In 2022 the [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) (PISA) ranked the overall knowledge and skills of British 15-year-olds as 14th in the world in reading, mathematics and science. The average British student scored 494, above the [OECD](33853117.md) average of 478.[^cite_note-437]&#x20;

### Health

Main articles: [Health in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [Healthcare in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Healthcare in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_England), [Healthcare in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Scotland), [Healthcare in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Wales), and [Healthcare in Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Northern_Ireland)



[NHS Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Scotland)'s [Queen Elizabeth University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_University_Hospital) in Glasgow, the largest hospital campus in Europe

The modern system of [universal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_service) [publicly funded healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care) in the United Kingdom has its origins in the establishment of the [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service) (NHS), which began operating in 1948 as the primary healthcare provider in the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-438] The widespread popularity of the NHS has led to it being described as a "national religion".[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440] Healthcare in the United Kingdom is a [devolved matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) and each country has its own system of universal publicly funded healthcare ([NHS in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_in_England), [NHS Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Scotland), [Health and Social Care in Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_and_Social_Care_in_Northern_Ireland) and [NHS Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Wales)), although private healthcare is also available. Public healthcare is provided to all [UK permanent residents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_nationality_law) and is mostly free at the point of need, being paid for from [general taxation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_Kingdom). The [World Health Organization](33583.md), in 2000, ranked the provision of healthcare in the United Kingdom as fifteenth-best in Europe and eighteenth in the world.[^cite_note-441]&#x20;

Since 1979 expenditure on healthcare has increased significantly.[^cite_note-442] The 2018 [OECD](33853117.md) data, which incorporates in health a chunk of what in the UK is classified as social care, has the UK spending £3,121 per person.[^cite_note-443] In 2017 the UK spent £2,989 per person on healthcare, near the median for members of the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development.[^cite_note-444]&#x20;

Regulatory bodies are organised on a UK-wide basis such as the [General Medical Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Medical_Council), the [Nursing and Midwifery Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursing_and_Midwifery_Council) and non-governmental-based, such as the [Royal Colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_royal_college). Political and operational responsibility for healthcare lies with four national [governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governments); healthcare in England is the responsibility of the UK Government; healthcare in Northern Ireland is the responsibility of the Northern Ireland Executive; healthcare in Scotland is the responsibility of the Scottish Government; and healthcare in Wales is the responsibility of the Welsh Government.[^cite_note-445]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Culture of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_England), [Culture of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Scotland), [Culture of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Wales), and [Culture of Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Northern_Ireland)

The culture of the United Kingdom is influenced by the nation's island status, its history, and being a [political union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_union) of four countries with each preserving distinctive traditions, customs and symbolism. British influence can be observed in the language, culture and [legal systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) of many of [its former colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_that_have_gained_independence_from_the_United_Kingdom), in particular the United States, Australia, Canada, New Zealand and Ireland, a common culture known as the Anglosphere.[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson2004307-447] Most of its former colonies are Commonwealth countries, bonded by a shared British inheritance.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] A global survey in 2024 ranked the UK third in the 'Most Influential Countries' rankings, behind the US and China.[^cite_note-451]&#x20;

### Literature and drama

Main articles: [British literature](296550.md) and [Theatre of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Literature of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_England), [Scottish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_literature), [Welsh literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_literature), and [Literature of Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_Northern_Ireland)

Robert Burns

[Robert Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Burns) (1759–1796)

William Shakespeare

[William Shakespeare](32897.md) (1564–1616)

Burns and Shakespeare are considered the [national poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_poet) of Scotland and England respectively.

British literature includes that associated with the United Kingdom, the [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man) and the [Channel Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Islands). Most British literature is in English. In 2022, 669 million physical books were sold in the UK, the most ever.[^cite_note-452] Britain is renowned for [children's literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_for_children); writers include [Daniel Defoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Defoe), [Rudyard Kipling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudyard_Kipling), [Lewis Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Carroll) and [Beatrix Potter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatrix_Potter), who also illustrated her own books. Other writers include [A. A. Milne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._A._Milne), [Enid Blyton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enid_Blyton), [J. R. R. Tolkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._R._R._Tolkien), [Roald Dahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roald_Dahl), [Terry Pratchett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Pratchett) and [J. K. Rowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._K._Rowling), who wrote *[Harry Potter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Potter)*, the [best-selling book series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_book_series) of all time.[^cite_note-453]&#x20;

The playwright and poet [William Shakespeare](32897.md) is generally regarded as the greatest dramatist ever and the [national poet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_poet) of England.[^cite_note-454] Theatre of United Kingdom plays an important part in British culture and has had a vibrant tradition of [theatre](20913771.md) since the Renaissance with roots going back to the Roman occupation. Other important figures in the literature of England are [Geoffrey Chaucer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Chaucer), known for *[The Canterbury Tales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Canterbury_Tales)*, the poet [William Wordsworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wordsworth), and other [Romantic poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_Poetry), also the novelists [Charles Dickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dickens), [H. G. Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._G._Wells), [George Orwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Orwell), [Aldous Huxley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldous_Huxley) and [Ian Fleming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Fleming). The 20th-century English crime writer [Agatha Christie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agatha_Christie) is the [best-selling novelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_fiction_authors) in history.[^cite_note-455] Twelve of the top 25 of 100 novels by British writers chosen by a BBC poll of global critics were written by women; these included works by [George Eliot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Eliot), [Virginia Woolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Woolf), [Charlotte Brontë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Bront%C3%AB), [Emily Brontë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_Bront%C3%AB), [Mary Shelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Shelley), [Jane Austen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Austen), [Doris Lessing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doris_Lessing) and [Zadie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadie_Smith).[^cite_note-456]&#x20;

Scottish literature includes [Arthur Conan Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Conan_Doyle) (the creator of [Sherlock Holmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherlock_Holmes)), [Sir Walter Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Scott), [J. M. Barrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._Barrie), [Robert Louis Stevenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Louis_Stevenson) (whose novel *[Treasure Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasure_Island)* strongly influenced the portrayal of [pirates in the arts and popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirates_in_the_arts_and_popular_culture)), and the poet [Robert Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Burns), who is considered the national poet of Scotland. More recently [Hugh MacDiarmid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_MacDiarmid) and [Neil M. Gunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_M._Gunn) contributed to the [Scottish Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Renaissance), with grimmer works from [Ian Rankin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Rankin) and [Iain Banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iain_Banks). Edinburgh was UNESCO's first worldwide [City of Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Literature).[^cite_note-457]&#x20;

Welsh literature includes Britain's oldest known poem, *[Y Gododdin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_Gododdin)*, most likely composed in the late 6th century. It was written in [Cumbric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumbric_language) or [Old Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Welsh) and contains the earliest known reference to [King Arthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Arthur).[^cite_note-458] The [Arthurian legend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthurian_legend) was further developed by [Geoffrey of Monmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_of_Monmouth).[^cite_note-459] [Dafydd ap Gwilym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dafydd_ap_Gwilym) (*fl.* 1320–1370) is widely regarded as one of the greatest European poets of his age.[^cite_note-460] [Daniel Owen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Owen) is credited as the first Welsh-language novelist, publishing *[Rhys Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhys_Lewis_\(novel\))* in 1885. The best-known of the [Anglo-Welsh poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Welsh_poetry) are [Dylan Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dylan_Thomas) and [R. S. Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._S._Thomas), the latter nominated for the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) in 1996. Leading Welsh novelists of the twentieth century include [Richard Llewellyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Llewellyn) and [Kate Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Roberts_\(author\)).[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462]&#x20;

Northern Ireland's most popular writer is [C. S. Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._S._Lewis), who was born in Belfast and wrote *[The Chronicles of Narnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chronicles_of_Narnia)*.[^cite_note-463] Irish writers, living at a time when all of Ireland was part of the United Kingdom, include [Oscar Wilde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Wilde),[^cite_note-464] [Bram Stoker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bram_Stoker) (who wrote *[Dracula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dracula)*)[^cite_note-465] and [George Bernard Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Bernard_Shaw).[^cite_note-466] There have been many authors whose origins were from outside the United Kingdom but who moved to the UK, including [Joseph Conrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Conrad),[^cite_note-467] [T. S. Eliot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._S._Eliot),[^cite_note-468] [Kazuo Ishiguro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazuo_Ishiguro),[^cite_note-469] Sir [Salman Rushdie](28722.md)[^cite_note-470] and [Ezra Pound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Pound).[^cite_note-471]&#x20;

### Philosophy

Main article: [British philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_philosophy)

The United Kingdom is famous for "[British Empiricism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empiricism)", a branch of the philosophy that states that only knowledge verified by experience is valid, and 'Scottish Philosophy', sometimes referred to as the '[Scottish School of Common Sense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_School_of_Common_Sense)'.[^cite_note-472] The most famous philosophers of British Empiricism are [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke), [George Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Berkeley)[^cite_note-473] and [David Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hume); while [Dugald Stewart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dugald_Stewart), [Thomas Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Reid) and [William Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_William_Hamilton,_9th_Baronet) were major exponents of the Scottish "common sense" school. Two Britons are also notable for the ethical theory of [utilitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilitarianism), a moral philosophy first used by [Jeremy Bentham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Bentham) and later by [John Stuart Mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill) in his short work *[Utilitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilitarianism_\(book\))*.[^cite_note-474]&#x20;

### Media

Main articles: [Media of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [Censorship in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Media of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_England), [Media of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Scotland), [Media of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Wales), and [Media of Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Northern_Ireland)

The [BBC](19344654.md), founded in 1922, is the United Kingdom's publicly funded radio, television and Internet broadcasting corporation, and is the oldest and largest broadcaster in the world.[^cite_note-MediaNewsline-475][^cite_note-ProspectMag-476][^cite_note-AboutBBC-477] It operates television and radio stations across the UK and abroad and its domestic services are funded by the [television licence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_licensing_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-478] The [BBC World Service](37627.md) is an [international broadcaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_broadcasting) owned and operated by the BBC, and the world's largest.[^cite_note-479] It broadcasts radio news, speech and discussions in more than 40 languages.[^cite_note-480]&#x20;

Other major players in the UK media include [ITV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_plc), which operates 11 of the 15 regional television broadcasters that make up the [ITV Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_\(TV_network\)),[^cite_note-481] and [Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_UK).[^cite_note-482] Newspapers produced in the United Kingdom include the *[Daily Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Mail)*, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*, *[The Telegraph](23797577.md)*, *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)*, and the *[Financial Times](136566.md)*.[^cite_note-483] Magazines and journals published in the United Kingdom that have achieved worldwide circulation include *[The Spectator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spectator)*, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*, *[New Statesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Statesman)* and *[Radio Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Times)*.&#x20;



[MediaCityUK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MediaCityUK) in [Salford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salford), [Greater Manchester](79819.md), is one of the largest media production facilities in Europe.

London dominates the media sector in the UK: national newspapers and television and radio are largely based there, although [MediaCityUK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MediaCityUK) in Manchester is also a significant national media centre. Edinburgh and Glasgow, and Cardiff, are important centres of newspaper and broadcasting production in Scotland and Wales, respectively.[^cite_note-484] The UK publishing sector, including books, directories and databases, journals, magazines and business media, newspapers and news agencies, has a combined turnover of around £20 billion and employs 167,000 people.[^cite_note-485] In 2015 the UK published 2,710 book titles per million inhabitants, more than any other country, with much of this exported to other [Anglophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglophone) countries.[^cite_note-486]&#x20;

In 2010, 82.5 per cent of the UK population were Internet users, the [highest proportion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_Internet_users) amongst the 20 countries with the largest total number of users in that year.[^cite_note-487] The [British video game industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_the_United_Kingdom) is the largest in Europe, and since 2022 the UK has the [largest video game market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_video_games_markets_by_country) in Europe by sales, surpassing [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_Germany).[^cite_note-488] It is the world's third-largest producer of video games after [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_Japan) and the [United States](31669618.md).[^cite_note-489]&#x20;

### Visual art

Main articles: [Art of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [Architecture of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_the_United_Kingdom)



The [Angel of the North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angel_of_the_North) sculpture by [Antony Gormley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Gormley) has become a symbol of [Northern England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_England).

Major British artists include the [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) artists [William Blake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Blake), [John Constable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Constable), [Samuel Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Palmer), and [J. M. W. Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._W._Turner); the [portrait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portrait) painters [Sir Joshua Reynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Reynolds) and [Lucian Freud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucian_Freud); the landscape artists [Thomas Gainsborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Gainsborough) and [L. S. Lowry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._S._Lowry); the pioneer of the [Arts and Crafts movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_and_Crafts_movement) [William Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Morris); the figurative painter [Francis Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Bacon_\(artist\)); the [Pop artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_artist) [Peter Blake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Blake_\(artist\)), [Richard Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Hamilton_\(artist\)) and [David Hockney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hockney); the pioneers of [conceptual art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conceptual_art) movement [Art & Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_%26_Language);[^cite_note-490] the collaborative duo [Gilbert and George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilbert_and_George); the [abstract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art) artist [Howard Hodgkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Hodgkin); and the sculptors [Antony Gormley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Gormley), [Anish Kapoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anish_Kapoor) and [Henry Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Moore). During the late 1980s and 1990s the [Saatchi Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saatchi_Gallery) in London helped to bring to public attention a group of multi-genre artists who would become known as the "[Young British Artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_British_Artists)": [Damien Hirst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damien_Hirst), [Chris Ofili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Ofili), [Rachel Whiteread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Whiteread), [Tracey Emin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracey_Emin), [Mark Wallinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Wallinger), [Steve McQueen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_McQueen_\(director\)), [Sam Taylor-Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Taylor-Wood) and the [Chapman Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_and_Dinos_Chapman) are amongst the better-known members of this loosely affiliated movement.&#x20;

The [Royal Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy) in London is a key organisation for the promotion of the visual arts in the United Kingdom. Major schools of art in the UK include: the six-school [University of the Arts London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Arts_London), which includes the [Central Saint Martins College of Art and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Saint_Martins_College_of_Art_and_Design) and [Chelsea College of Art and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_College_of_Art_and_Design); [Goldsmiths, University of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldsmiths,_University_of_London); the [Slade School of Fine Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slade_School_of_Fine_Art) (part of [University College London](52029.md)); the [Glasgow School of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow_School_of_Art); the [Royal College of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_College_of_Art); and [The Ruskin School of Drawing and Fine Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ruskin_School_of_Drawing_and_Fine_Art) (part of the University of Oxford). The [Courtauld Institute of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtauld_Institute_of_Art) is a leading centre for the teaching of the [history of art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_art). Important art galleries in the United Kingdom include the [National Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery), [National Portrait Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Portrait_Gallery_\(London\)), [Tate Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tate_Britain), and [Tate Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tate_Modern) (the most-visited modern art gallery in the world, with around 4.7 million visitors per year).[^cite_note-491]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_the_United_Kingdom)

Further information: [Music of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_England_\(disambiguation\)), [Music of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Scotland), [Music of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Wales), and [Music of Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Northern_Ireland)



[The Proms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Proms) is a classical music festival, most closely associated with [Henry Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Wood) and held at the [Royal Albert Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Hall) in London, which regularly plays music by [Edward Elgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Elgar) and other British composers.

Various styles of music have become popular in the UK, including the [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) of [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music_of_England), [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music_of_Scotland), [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Wales#Folk_music) and [Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music_of_Northern_Ireland). English folk features melodic ballads with strong lyrics and music for country dancing often using accordion and fiddles.[^cite_note-492] Scottish folk features bagpipes and fiddles playing traditional dance tunes with bouncy tempos.[^cite_note-493] Welsh folk has harps and vocal harmonies often sung in Welsh.[^cite_note-494] Northern Irish folk blends fiddles with flutes merging Scottish and Irish influences.[^cite_note-495]&#x20;

Historically, there has been [Renaissance music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_music) from the [Tudor period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_period), with [masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_\(music\)), [madrigals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrigal) and [lute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lute) music by [Thomas Tallis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Tallis), [John Taverner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Taverner), [William Byrd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Byrd), [Orlando Gibbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Gibbons) and [John Dowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dowland). After the [Stuart Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart_Restoration), an English tradition of dramatic [masques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masque), anthems and airs became established, led by [Henry Purcell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Purcell), followed by [Thomas Arne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Arne) and others. [George Frideric Handel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Frideric_Handel) composed the anthem *[Zadok the Priest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadok_the_Priest)* for the [coronation of George II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_George_II); it became the traditional ceremonial music for anointing all future monarchs. Handel's many oratorios, such as his famous *[Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_\(Handel\))*, were written in English.[^cite_note-496]&#x20;

In the second half of the 19th century, [Arthur Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Sullivan) and his librettist [W. S. Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._S._Gilbert) wrote their popular [Savoy operas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savoy_opera), and [Edward Elgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Elgar) composed a wide range of music. Increasingly, composers became inspired by the English countryside and its [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music), notably [Gustav Holst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Holst), [Ralph Vaughan Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Vaughan_Williams) and [Benjamin Britten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Britten), a pioneer of modern British opera. Amongst the many post-war composers, some of the most notable have made their own personal choice of musical identity: [Peter Maxwell Davies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Maxwell_Davies) (Orkney), [Harrison Birtwistle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrison_Birtwistle) (mythological), and [John Tavener](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Tavener) (religious).[^cite_note-497] Recent classical singers include [Alfie Boe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfie_Boe), [Bryn Terfel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryn_Terfel), [Katherine Jenkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katherine_Jenkins), [Michael Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Ball), [Roderick Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roderick_Williams), [Russell Watson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Watson) and [Sarah Brightman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Brightman), while [Nicola Benedetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_Benedetti) and [Nigel Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Kennedy) are renowned for their violin ability.[^cite_note-498]&#x20;

According to *[The New Grove Dictionary of Music and Musicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Grove_Dictionary_of_Music_and_Musicians)* the term "[pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music)" originated in Britain in the mid-1950s to describe [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll)'s fusion with the "new youth music".[^cite_note-499] *The Oxford Dictionary of Music* states that artists such as [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) and [the Rolling Stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rolling_Stones) drove pop music to the forefront of popular music in the early 1960s.[^cite_note-500] Birmingham became known as the birthplace of [heavy metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_metal_music), with the band [Black Sabbath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sabbath) starting there in the 1960s.[^cite_note-501] In the following years, Britain took part in the development of [rock music](25423.md), with British acts pioneering [hard rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_rock),[^cite_note-502] [raga rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga_rock), heavy metal,[^cite_note-503] [space rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_rock), [glam rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glam_rock),[^cite_note-504] [Gothic rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_rock),[^cite_note-505] [psychedelic rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychedelic_rock)[^cite_note-506] and [punk rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock).[^cite_note-507] British acts also developed [neo soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo_soul) and created [dubstep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubstep).[^cite_note-508] The UK has produced some of the most prominent English-speaking [rappers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rappers) along with the United States, including [Stormzy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormzy), [Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_\(rapper\)), [Yxng Bane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yxng_Bane), [Ramz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramz_\(rapper\)), [Little Simz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Simz) and [Skepta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skepta).[^cite_note-509]&#x20;



The [OVO Hydro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OVO_Hydro) in [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow), Scotland, a 14,500-capacity arena consistently ranked within the top ten busiest venues worldwide

[^cite_note-510]

The Beatles have international sales of over 1 billion units and are the [biggest-selling band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_music_artists), in addition to being [widely regarded as the most influential band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_impact_of_the_Beatles) in the history of popular music.[^cite_note-Beatles_sales-511][^cite_note-McCartney-512][^cite_note-Guinness-513][^cite_note-514] Other prominent British contributors to popular music over the last 50 years include the Rolling Stones, [Pink Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_Floyd), [Queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_\(band\)), [Led Zeppelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Led_Zeppelin), the [Bee Gees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bee_Gees) and [Elton John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elton_John), all of whom have worldwide record sales of 200 million or more.[^cite_note-515] The [Brit Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_Awards) are the [BPI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Phonographic_Industry)'s annual music awards, and some of the British recipients of the [Outstanding Contribution to Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_Award_for_Outstanding_Contribution_to_Music) award include [the Who](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Who), [David Bowie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bowie), [Eric Clapton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Clapton), [Rod Stewart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Stewart), [the Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Police) and [Fleetwood Mac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleetwood_Mac) (who are a British-American band).[^cite_note-516] More recent UK music acts that have had international success include [George Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Michael), [Oasis](161515.md), [Spice Girls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice_Girls), [Radiohead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiohead), [Coldplay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coldplay), [Arctic Monkeys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Monkeys), [Robbie Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robbie_Williams), [Amy Winehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Winehouse), [Susan Boyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Boyle), [Adele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adele), [Ed Sheeran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Sheeran), [Lewis Capaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Capaldi), [One Direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Direction), [Harry Styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Styles) and [Dua Lipa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dua_Lipa).[^cite_note-517] As of 2024 the UK is the world's [third-largest music market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_music_markets) behind the US and Japan,[^cite_note-518] and in 2021 was the second-largest exporter of music behind the US.[^cite_note-519]&#x20;

Many British cities are known for their music. Acts from Liverpool have had 54 UK chart number 1 hit singles, more per capita than any other city worldwide.[^cite_note-520] Glasgow's contribution was recognised in 2008 when it was named a [City of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Music_\(UNESCO\)) by [UNESCO](21786641.md).[^cite_note-521] Manchester played a role in the spread of dance music such as [acid house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_house), and from the mid-1990s, [Britpop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britpop). London and Bristol are closely associated with the origins of [electronic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_music) sub-genres such as [drum and bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drum_and_bass) and [trip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trip_hop).[^cite_note-522]&#x20;

UK dance music traces its roots back to the black British [Sound System Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_system_\(Jamaican\)) and the [New Age Traveller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Age_travellers) movement of the 60s and 70s,[^cite_note-523] it also has influences from the [Chicago House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_music) and [Detroit techno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_techno) scenes. In the late 1980s, dance music exploded with [Rave](43488.md) culture mainly [Acid House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_House) tracks which were made mainstream with novelty records (such as Smart E's [Sesame's Treet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame%27s_Treet) and the Prodigy's [Charly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charly_\(song\)))[^cite_note-524] and the [Balearic sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balearic_beat) brought back from the Ibiza club scene. This led on to genres such as [UK Garage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Garage), [Speed Garage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_Garage), [Drum and bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drum_and_bass), [Jungle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jungle_music), [Trance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trance_music), and [Dubstep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubstep). Influential UK dance acts past and present include [808 State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/808_State), [Orbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_\(band\)), [the Prodigy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Prodigy), [Underworld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underworld_\(band\)), [Roni Size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roni_Size), [Leftfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftfield), [Massive Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_Attack), [Groove Armada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groove_Armada), [Fatboy Slim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatboy_Slim), [Faithless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faithless), [Basement Jaxx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basement_Jaxx), [Chemical Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_Brothers), [Disclosure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disclosure_\(band\)), [Calvin Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvin_Harris), and [Fred Again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Again).[^cite_note-525] Other influential UK DJs include [Judge Jules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judge_Jules), [Pete Tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Tong), [Carl Cox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Cox), [Paul Oakenfold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Oakenfold), [John Digweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Digweed) and [Sasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasha_\(dj\)).[^cite_note-526]&#x20;

### Cinema

Main articles: [Cinema of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_the_United_Kingdom) and [Independent cinema in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_cinema_in_the_United_Kingdom)

The United Kingdom has had a considerable influence on the history of the cinema. The British directors [Alfred Hitchcock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Hitchcock), whose film *[Vertigo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertigo_\(film\))* is considered by some critics as the [best film of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_considered_the_best),[^cite_note-527] and [David Lean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lean), who directed *[Lawrence of Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_of_Arabia_\(film\))*, are amongst the most critically acclaimed directors ever.[^cite_note-528] Recent popular directors include [Christopher Nolan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Nolan), [Sam Mendes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Mendes), [Steve McQueen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_McQueen_\(director\)), [Richard Curtis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Curtis), [Danny Boyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Boyle), [Tony Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Scott) and [Ridley Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Scott).[^cite_note-529][^cite_note-530][^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532] Many British actors have achieved international fame and critical success. Some of the most commercially successful films have been produced in the United Kingdom, including two of the [highest-grossing film franchises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest-grossing_film_series) (*[Harry Potter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Potter_\(film_series\))* and *[James Bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bond_\(film_series\))*).[^cite_note-533]&#x20;

2019 was a particularly good year for British films which grossed around £10.3 billion globally, accounting for 28.7 per cent of global box office revenue.[^cite_note-534] UK box-office takings totalled £1.25 billion in 2019, with around 176 million admissions.[^cite_note-535] In 2023 UK film and television studio stage space stood at 6.9 million sq ft, with 1 million sq ft added in the past year with more in development.[^cite_note-536] The annual [BAFTA Film Awards](150200.md) are hosted by the [British Academy of Film and Television Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Academy_of_Film_and_Television_Arts).[^cite_note-537]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [British cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_cuisine)

Further information: [English cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_cuisine), [Scottish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_cuisine), [Welsh cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_cuisine), and [Northern Irish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Irish_cuisine)



[Fish and chips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_and_chips), a traditional British dish, served with lemon, tartar sauce and mushy peas

British cuisine developed from influences reflective of its land, settlements, [arrivals of new settlers and immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_the_United_Kingdom), trade and colonialism. The food of England has historically been characterised by simplicity of approach and a reliance on the high quality of natural produce.[^cite_note-538] The traditional [Sunday roast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunday_roast) is one example, featuring a [roast joint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roasting) (usually of beef), lamb, chicken, or pork, often [free range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_range) (and generally grass-fed, in the case of beef). Roasts are served with either roasted or boiled vegetables, [Yorkshire pudding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_pudding) and [gravy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravy). Other traditional meals include [meat pies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat_pie) and [stews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stew). A poll by [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) in 2019 rated classic British food, with more than 80 per cent liking the Sunday roast, Yorkshire pudding, [fish and chips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_and_chips), [crumpets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crumpet) and the [full English breakfast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_English_breakfast).[^cite_note-539]&#x20;

The UK is home to a large selection of [fine dining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fine_dining). The nation hosts multiple restaurant guides such as *[The Good Food Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Good_Food_Guide)* and *[Harden's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harden%27s)*. In 2025 there were 197 restaurants with a [Michelin Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Star); [ten of which have received a three-star rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Michelin_3-star_restaurants_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-540] Sweet foods are common within British cuisine, and there is a long list of [British desserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_British_desserts). Afternoon tea is a light afternoon meal served with tea in tea rooms and hotels around the United Kingdom, with the tradition dating back to around 1840.[^cite_note-541] A poll from July 2024 revealed that 3 per cent of the UK population follow a [vegan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegan) diet, 6 per cent are [vegetarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetarian), and 13 per cent identify as [flexitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flexitarian) (following a mainly vegetarian diet).[^cite_note-542] The British Empire facilitated knowledge of [Indian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_cuisine) with its "strong, penetrating spices and herbs". British cuisine has absorbed the cultural influence of those who have settled in Britain, producing hybrid dishes, such as [chicken tikka masala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_tikka_masala).[^cite_note-543]&#x20;

The UK has many [gastropubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastropub) and is the birthplace of many alcoholic drinks including several beer styles such as [pale ale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pale_ale), [India pale ale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_pale_ale), [bitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitter_\(beer\)), [brown ale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_ale), [porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porter_\(beer\)), and [stout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stout). The number of [craft beers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craft_beer) and microbreweries has expanded rapidly in the last two decades.[^cite_note-544] Other popular alcoholic drinks produced in the UK include [Scotch whisky](28896.md), [English wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine_from_the_United_Kingdom), [gin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gin), [perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry) and [cider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cider).&#x20;

### Sport

Main articles: [Sport in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [Great Britain at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain_at_the_Olympics)

Further information: [Sport in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_England), [Sport in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Scotland), [Sport in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Wales), and [Sport in Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Northern_Ireland)



The [2023 FA Cup final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_FA_Cup_final) at [Wembley Stadium](8913012.md) between [Manchester City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_City) and [Manchester United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_United)



Golf originated from the [Old Course at St Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Course_at_St_Andrews) in Scotland.

[Association football](10568.md), [tennis](29773.md), [table tennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_tennis), [badminton](3956.md), [rugby union](25405.md), [rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_league), [rugby sevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_sevens), [golf](19568112.md), [boxing](4243.md), [netball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netball), [water polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo), [field hockey](10886.md), [billiards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_billiards), [darts](397713.md), [rowing](25736.md), [rounders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rounders) and [cricket](25675557.md) originated or were substantially developed in the UK, with the rules and codes of many modern sports invented and codified in the Victorian era.[^cite_note-547]&#x20;

A poll in 2003 found that football is the most popular sport in the UK.[^cite_note-sports_poll-548] England is recognised by [FIFA](11049.md) as the birthplace of club football, and the [Football Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Association) is the oldest of its kind, with the [rules of football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_the_Game_\(association_football\)) first drafted in 1863 by [Ebenezer Cobb Morley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebenezer_Cobb_Morley).[^cite_note-549] Each of the [Home Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Nations) (England, Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland) has its own football association, national team and [league system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_system), and each is individually a governing member of the [International Football Association Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Football_Association_Board) alongside FIFA. The English top division, the [Premier League](11250.md), is the most watched football league in the world.[^cite_note-550] The first international football match was contested by [England](9904.md) and [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_national_football_team) on 30 November 1872.[^cite_note-551] England, Scotland, [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wales_national_football_team) and [Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_national_football_team) usually compete as separate countries in international competitions.[^cite_note-552]&#x20;

In 2003 rugby union was ranked the second-most-popular sport in the UK.[^cite_note-sports_poll-548] The sport was created in [Rugby School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_School), Warwickshire, and the [first rugby international](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1871_England_versus_Scotland_rugby_union_match) took place on 27 March 1871 between [England](691024.md) and [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_national_rugby_union_team).[^cite_note-553][^cite_note-554] England, Scotland, [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wales_national_rugby_union_team), [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ireland_national_rugby_union_team), [France](955656.md) and [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy_national_rugby_union_team) compete in the [Six Nations Championship](26547.md), the premier international rugby union tournament in the northern hemisphere. [Sports governing bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_governing_bodies) in [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union_in_England), [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Scotland), [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Wales) and [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Ireland) organise and regulate the game separately.[^cite_note-555] Every four years the Home Nations make a combined team known as the [British and Irish Lions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_and_Irish_Lions) which tours Australia, New Zealand and South Africa.&#x20;

The United Kingdom hosted the [Summer Olympic Games](27566.md) in [1908](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1908_Summer_Olympics), [1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Summer_Olympics) and [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics), with London acting as the host city on all three occasions. Birmingham hosted the [2022 Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Commonwealth_Games), the seventh time a country of the United Kingdom hosted the [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games) (England, Scotland and Wales have each hosted the Commonwealth Games at least once).[^cite_note-556]&#x20;

### Symbols

Main article: [Symbols of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbols_of_the_United_Kingdom,_the_Channel_Islands_and_the_Isle_of_Man)



Union Jack flags on [The Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mall,_London), London

The [flag of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom) is the [Union Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Flag), which is also referred to as the Union Jack.[^cite_note-557] It was created in 1606 by the superimposition of the [flag of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_England), representing [Saint George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_George), on the [flag of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Scotland), representing [Saint Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Andrew), and was updated in 1801 with the addition of [Saint Patrick's Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Flag).[^cite_note-558] Wales is not represented in the Union Flag, as Wales had been conquered and [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexed) to England prior to the formation of the United Kingdom. The possibility of redesigning the Union Flag to include representation of Wales was discussed in 2007.[^cite_note-559] The [national anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_anthem) of the United Kingdom is "[God Save the King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_Save_the_King)", with "King" replaced with "Queen" in the lyrics whenever the monarch is a woman.&#x20;

[England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_England), [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Wales) and [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Scotland) each have their own national symbols, including their national flags. [Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Northern_Ireland) also has symbols, [many of which are shared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Ireland,_the_Republic_of_Ireland_and_Northern_Ireland) with the [Republic of Ireland](14560.md), but has no official flag[^cite_note-minahan-560]. Beside [the Lion and the Unicorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lion_and_the_Unicorn) and the [Welsh Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Dragon) of heraldry, the [bulldog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulldog) is also used and commonly represented with the Union Flag.[^cite_note-561] Great Britain, rather than the United Kingdom as a whole, also has [personifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_personification) representing it, two of which are [Britannia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britannia)[^cite_note-562][^cite_note-Collins-563] and [John Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bull).[^cite_note-564]&#x20;

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;"God Save the King" is the [national anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_anthem) by custom, not statute, and there is no authorised version. Typically only the first verse is usually sung, although the second verse is also often sung as well at state and public events.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-1)</sup> The words *King, he, him, his*, used at present, are replaced by *Queen, she, her* when the monarch is female.

[^cite_note-5]: &#x20;Scots, Ulster Scots, Welsh, Cornish, Scottish Gaelic and Irish are classed as [regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_language) or [minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_language) languages under the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe)'s [European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Charter_for_Regional_or_Minority_Languages).<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-reglang-3)</sup> These include defined obligations to promote those languages.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-4)</sup> See also [Languages of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_United_Kingdom). Welsh has limited *[de jure](43594.md)* official status in Wales, as well as in the provision of national government services provided for Wales.

[^cite_note-UK-wide2021-6]: &#x20;Whereas the censuses of England and Wales and of Northern Ireland went ahead as planned in 2021, the census in Scotland was postponed until 2022. The Office for National Statistics reports that these UK-wide statistics "have been created to provide an image of how UK census data may have looked if the most recent UK censuses had been conducted with an aligned date of 21 March 2021".

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;Although the United Kingdom has traditionally been seen as a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state), an alternative description of the UK as a "union state", put forward by, amongst others, [Vernon Bogdanor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernon_Bogdanor),<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-9)</sup> has become increasingly influential since the adoption of devolution in the 1990s.<sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-10)</sup> A union state is considered to differ from a unitary state in that while it maintains a central authority it also recognises the authority of historic rights and infrastructures of its component parts.<sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-11)</sup>

[^cite_note-ONSLandArea-13]: &#x20;[ONS](324029.md) Standard Area Measurement, "area to mean high water excluding inland water". Excludes the [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies) and [British Overseas Territories](207114.md).

[^cite_note-ONSArea-14]: &#x20;[ONS](324029.md) Standard Area Measurement, 'area to mean high water'. Excludes the [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies) and [British Overseas Territories](207114.md).

[^cite_note-20]: &#x20;Commonly also known as the "pound" (the name of [its base unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pound_\(currency\))) or "pound sterling". Some of the devolved countries, [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependency) and [British Overseas Territories](207114.md) issue their own sterling banknotes or currencies, or use another nation's currency. See [List of British currencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_British_currencies).

[^cite_note-21]: &#x20;Also observed by the [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies). For further information, see [Time in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_in_the_United_Kingdom).

[^cite_note-23]: &#x20;The UK Government uses the [ISO 8601](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_8601) format, yyyy-mm-dd, for machine-readable dates and times.<sup>[\[14\]](#cite_note-22)</sup> See [Date and time notation in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_and_time_notation_in_the_United_Kingdom).

[^cite_note-24]: &#x20;Excludes most overseas territories. See [Telephone numbers in the United Kingdom#Telephone numbers in Overseas Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_the_United_Kingdom#Telephone_numbers_in_Overseas_Territories).

[^cite_note-25]: &#x20;The [.gb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/.gb) domain is also reserved for the UK, but has been little used.

[^cite_note-29]: &#x20;Usage is mixed. *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*<sup>[\[15\]](#cite_note-26)</sup> and *[The Telegraph](23797577.md)*<sup>[\[16\]](#cite_note-27)</sup> use *Britain* as a synonym for the United Kingdom. The British [Cabinet Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_Office)'s [Government Digital Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Digital_Service) style guide<sup>[\[17\]](#cite_note-28)</sup> for use on [gov.uk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gov.uk) recommends: "Use UK and United Kingdom in preference to Britain and British (UK business, UK foreign policy, ambassador and high commissioner). But British embassy, not UK embassy."

[^cite_note-30]: &#x20;The [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man), [Guernsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bailiwick_of_Guernsey) and [Jersey](15693.md) are [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies) and not part of the UK.

[^cite_note-32]: &#x20;The United Kingdom does not have a codified constitution but an unwritten one formed of Acts of Parliament, court judgments, traditions, and conventions.<sup>[\[18\]](#cite_note-31)</sup>

[^cite_note-33]: &#x20;Compare to section 1 of both of the 1800 [Acts of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_\(1800\)) which reads: "the kingdoms of Great Britain and Ireland shall ... be united into one kingdom, by the name of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland".

[^cite_note-49]: &#x20;"UK" embassies became "British" embassies in 1961.<sup>[\[33\]](#cite_note-48)</sup>

[^cite_note-56]: &#x20;Historically, the term *British* was used to refer to members and institutions within the British Empire and later Commonwealth and was not limited to the geographical British Isles. The UK Government adopted the term for its exclusive use only in 1961, but in recognition of its wider usage first sought the prior consent of Australia, Canada and New Zealand.<sup>[\[38\]](#cite_note-54)</sup><sup>[\[39\]](#cite_note-55)</sup>

[^cite_note-179]: &#x20;British [sovereignty](28791.md) derives from the Crown, a [corporation sole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporation_sole) occupied by the monarch. It is therefore by and through the monarch that Parliament exercises supreme legislative authority over both the executive and judiciary. Distinguished Professor of [Public Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Law) Maurice Sunkin opined the Crown symbolically occupies "...what in other places would be a core element of a written constitution."<sup>[\[160\]](#cite_note-177)</sup> As a result of this state of constitutional affairs, the monarch is formally referred to as "[the Sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign)" in legislation.<sup>[\[161\]](#cite_note-178)</sup>

[^cite_note-187]: &#x20;For instance, the monarch alone appoints a member of Parliament as prime minister based on their ability to command the confidence of the House of Commons. The monarch also regularly confers [state honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orders,_decorations,_and_medals_of_the_United_Kingdom) in the personal gift of the Crown. In exceptional circumstances, the monarch may dismiss the prime minister, refuse a request to [dissolve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_Parliament) or [prorogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prorogation_in_the_United_Kingdom) Parliament, withhold [royal assent](153067.md) to [primary legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Parliament_\(UK\)), and prevent illegal use of the [British Armed Forces](31730.md), amongst other reserve powers.<sup>[\[168\]](#cite_note-186)</sup>

[^cite_note-307]: &#x20;Car brands here are classed as British based on several of the following criteria: historical heritage, cultural significance, design and engineering base, manufacturing location, headquarters location, UK registered company (even with overseas investors).

[^cite_note-473]: &#x20;Berkeley is in fact Irish but was called a 'British empiricist' due to the territory of what is now known as the [Republic of Ireland](14560.md) being in the UK at the time.

[^cite_note-547]: &#x20;In 2012, the president of the IOC, [Jacques Rogge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Rogge), stated, "This great, sports-loving country is widely recognised as the birthplace of modern sport. It was here that the concepts of sportsmanship and fair play were first codified into clear rules and regulations. It was here that sport was included as an educational tool in the school curriculum."<sup>[\[524\]](#cite_note-545)</sup><sup>[\[525\]](#cite_note-546)</sup>

The UK Parliament is sovereign and the Scottish Parliament is subordinate. The White Paper had indicated that this was to be the approach taken in the legislation. The Scottish Parliament is not to be seen as a reflection of the settled will of the people of Scotland or of popular sovereignty but as a reflection of its subordination to a higher legal authority. Following the logic of this argument, the power of the Scottish Parliament to legislate can be withdrawn or overridden...; Elliot, M. (2004). ["United Kingdom: Parliamentary sovereignty under pressure"](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Ficon%2F2.3.545). *International Journal of Constitutional Law*. **2** (3): 545–627, 553–554. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/icon/2.3.545](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Ficon%2F2.3.545). Notwithstanding substantial differences among the schemes, an important common factor is that the UK Parliament has not renounced legislative sovereignty in relation to the three nations concerned. For example, the Scottish Parliament is empowered to enact primary legislation on all matters, save those in relation to which competence is explicitly denied ... but this power to legislate on what may be termed "devolved matters" is concurrent with the Westminster Parliament's general power to legislate for Scotland on any matter at all, including devolved matters ... In theory, therefore, Westminster may legislate on Scottish devolved matters whenever it chooses...

The British parliament has the power to abolish the Scottish parliament and the Welsh assembly by a simple majority vote in both houses, but since both were sanctioned by referenda, it would be politically difficult to abolish them without the sanction of a further vote by the people. In this way, several of the constitutional measures introduced by the Blair government appear to be entrenched and not subject to a simple exercise of parliamentary sovereignty at Westminster.

[^cite_note-221]: &#x20;Meehan, E. (1999). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/pa/52.1.19](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Fpa%2F52.1.19). \[T]he distinctive involvement of two governments in the Northern Irish problem means that Northern Ireland's new arrangements rest upon an intergovernmental agreement. If this can be equated with a treaty, it could be argued that the forthcoming distribution of power between Westminster and Belfast has similarities with divisions specified in the written constitutions of federal states...Although the Agreement makes the general proviso that Westminster's 'powers to make legislation for Northern Ireland' remains 'unaffected', without an explicit categorical reference to reserved matters, it may be more difficult than in Scotland or Wales for devolved powers to be repatriated. The retraction of devolved powers would not merely entail consultation in Northern Ireland backed implicitly by the absolute power of parliamentary sovereignty but also the renegotiation of an intergovernmental agreement.

[^cite_note-Keating21-231]: &#x20;Keating, Michael (2 February 2021). "Taking back control? Brexit and the territorial constitution of the United Kingdom". *[Journal of European Public Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_European_Public_Policy)*. **28** (4). Abingdon: [Taylor & Francis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_%26_Francis): 6–7. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1080/13501763.2021.1876156](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F13501763.2021.1876156). [hdl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hdl_\(identifier\)):[1814/70296](https://hdl.handle.net/1814%2F70296). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [234066376](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:234066376). The UK Internal Market Act gives ministers sweeping powers to enforce mutual recognition and non-discrimination across the four jurisdictions. Existing differences and some social and health matters are exempted but these are much less extensive than the exemptions permitted under the EU Internal Market provisions. Only after an amendment in the House of Lords, the Bill was amended to provide a weak and non-binding consent mechanism for amendments (equivalent to the Sewel Convention) to the list of exemptions. The result is that, while the devolved governments retain regulatory competences, these are undermined by the fact that goods and services originating in, or imported into, England can be marketed anywhere.
